# PMS–VECTOR

## Direction and Rotation

### *Praxeological Orientation under Perspectival Freedom, Reversibility, and Transformable Possibility*

**Author:** T. Zöller

---

## Abstract

PMS is a direction-bearing but non-final praxeological grammar: it can render differences in praxis structurally legible without thereby establishing a universal scale, final truth, or moral decision rule. PMS–VECTOR addresses the resulting problem of discriminative orientation: under what conditions can a structural difference warrant direction without converting local comparison into total order, perspectival dependence into relativism, framework coherence into authority, or practical effect into truth? As a late meta-reconstructive orientation paper, VECTOR retains a minimal core

\[
\boxed{
VECTOR:=\langle DIR,ROT\rangle,
\qquad
V_t(O\mid F,Q,\pi)=\langle DIR_t,RP_t\rangle,
}
\]

where DIR (Direction) expresses bounded structural direction and ROT (Rotation) denotes the admissible perspective-testing/discovery operation family; RP (ROT Profile) records the current rotation-test profile rather than treating an operation as a state value. The name VECTOR implies no vector space, scalar direction, geometric rotation, metric distance, or algebraic resultant. Direction is earned rather than presumed: vulnerability-driven ROT may strengthen, weaken, reverse, split, or collapse a DIR, while trade-off, incomparability, suspension, rival superiority, and non-capture remain legitimate results. The paper retains DIST (Distance) only as a derived shorthand for effective orientation–enactment separation after its own redundancy test finds no sufficient reason for third-component status.

VECTOR makes warrant sources, external premises, calibration thresholds, option evidence, Second-Order Agency mechanisms, consequence evidence, scene time, and analysis time explicit without promoting them into new core components. Bounded Test-Burden Closure permits a current inquiry to stop without implying final truth or endogenous threshold generation. Practical attribution is more strictly typed than structural robustness: PMS-LOGIC baseline attributability remains distinct from any VECTOR-mediated increase. The latter requires, where material, external-premise support, actor uptake, effective capacity, consequence relation, and role/time conditions. VECTOR therefore derives no categorical moral prescription, no person rank, and no increase in authority from stronger orientation. Under the required bridge, orientation may instead increase **structural attributability** or reveal constraint-relative practical necessity while leaving guilt, blame, liability, legitimacy, and moral obligation external. The architecture remains reducible; in the present paper that reduction is already real rather than hypothetical. DIST loses core status, “Adult Orientation” loses independent theoretical status and remains only supporting shorthand, and a same-burden non-PMS sensitivity model narrows the primary scope of DIR (Direction), ROT (Rotation), and standalone VECTOR for externally calibrated commensurable multi-criteria orientation.

---

# 1. Introduction — The Direction Problem

Praxeological Meta-Structure Theory (PMS) was developed as a generative grammar for rendering praxis structurally legible across difference, impulse, framing, non-event, attraction, asymmetry, temporality, recontextualization, distance, integration, and self-binding. Its strength lies in the ability to reconstruct heterogeneous forms of action through a shared operator grammar without reducing them to psychology, moral verdict, person-typing, or a single domain ontology. Yet PMS has also retained a deliberately non-final status. Its generative reach is not presented as proof of completeness; its portability is not proof of equivalence across domains; translation into its operator vocabulary is not proof of exhaustive capture; and internal coherence is not proof that the grammar discriminates better than its rivals.

This combination creates a problem that becomes sharper as PMS becomes more capable. A framework may remain explicitly non-final while nevertheless making distinctions that are not neutral. It may distinguish correction from reaction, truthful from false coordination, real from illusory capacity, reversible from sedimented trajectories, bounded adequacy from coherence without discrimination, or viable transformation from structural non-capture. These differences are not automatically rankings, and they do not authorize a universal scale. But neither are they empty. Some differences appear to carry direction.

The problem of PMS–VECTOR begins here. The question is not whether PMS can make praxis legible. That problem is already prior to VECTOR. Nor is the question whether PMS can be converted into a general theory of what is morally better. VECTOR rejects that move. Its question is narrower and more difficult:

\[
\boxed{\text{When can a structural difference warrant direction?}}
\]

A satisfactory answer must avoid two symmetrical failures. The first would be to convert every directional distinction into a total order: if one configuration is locally more adequate than another, the theory silently inflates that relation into a universal rank. The second would be to treat non-finality as if it erased all directional discrimination: because no final standpoint exists, no orientation can be better warranted than another. VECTOR proceeds from the claim that both moves are mistakes.

\[
\boxed{\text{Non-finality does not imply equivalence.}}
\]

At the same time:

\[
\boxed{\text{Direction does not imply total order.}}
\]

The task is therefore not to discover a hidden scalar beneath PMS. It is to reconstruct when bounded directional claims are warranted, how they change under perspectival pressure, how close they stand to enactment and binding, and when the correct result is not direction but trade-off, incomparability, suspension, or non-capture. This requires orientation to become testable rather than merely intuitive. A direction that survives only because its starting perspective remains unexamined has not yet earned robustness. Conversely, a direction that changes under rotation has not necessarily failed; the change itself may reveal a real dependence of the relation on role, time, asymmetry, frame, or consequence.

VECTOR therefore treats orientation not as an external verdict imposed on PMS, but as a late reconstruction of a problem already generated by PMS’s own combination of structural productivity and explicit non-finality. It asks whether distributed local direction can be made explicit enough to be compared, rotated, reduced, or refused without granting the resulting orientation any authority it has not separately earned.

## 1.1 Legibility without Equivalence

PMS is first of all a grammar of legibility. It proposes that heterogeneous praxis structures can be rendered explicit through a bounded operator vocabulary and that complex formations can be reconstructed through relations among those operators. Legibility, however, has never been identical to truth. A scene may be representable in PMS terms without PMS thereby exhausting what matters about the scene. A structure may be portable across domains without the domains becoming equivalent. A framework may cover a large range of cases without capturing every relevant difference among them.

Several distinctions therefore remain basic:

\[
\text{legibility}\neq\text{truth},
\]

\[
\text{portability}\neq\text{equivalence},
\]

\[
\text{coverage}\neq\text{capture},
\]

\[
\text{coherence}\neq\text{discrimination},
\]

\[
\text{implementation}\neq\text{proof}.
\]

These limits do more than restrain overclaim. They define the point at which a theory of orientation becomes necessary. If legibility were already truth, no further discriminative theory would be needed. If portability established equivalence, local comparison would be trivial. If coherence guaranteed discrimination, repeated success inside the framework would itself warrant directional confidence. PMS explicitly denies these shortcuts.

The resulting position is stronger than generic epistemic modesty. PMS can make claims that are structurally informative while leaving their completeness, comparative superiority, and rival adequacy open. It can therefore support meaningful differentiation without pretending that differentiation already supplies a final rank. This is the terrain in which VECTOR operates.

A directional claim requires more than the existence of a difference. Difference may be relevant without being orderable. Two structures may answer different questions, preserve different constraints, or carry losses that cannot responsibly be converted into a common scale. Conversely, the absence of a universal scale does not imply that every local comparison is impossible. A narrower claim may discriminate better than a broader one for a particular question; a coordination may preserve a real asymmetry more truthfully than another; a reconstruction may retain reference continuity where another loses it; one option may remain effectively actionable while another is only conceivable.

VECTOR therefore begins by refusing a false choice between total ranking and flat pluralism. Structural legibility can support bounded comparison without turning every comparison into a universal hierarchy. The relevant problem is not whether all things can be ranked, but whether a particular difference, under a declared reference and question, carries a direction that survives the pressure appropriate to that claim.

## 1.2 Non-Finality Does Not Imply Equivalence

Non-finality is sometimes treated as if it imposed a single epistemic consequence: because no account is final, all accounts must remain equally provisional and therefore, in the relevant sense, equally placed. This inference does not follow.

Two claims may both be revisable while differing substantially in discrimination, explanatory burden, reference preservation, consequence sensitivity, or robustness under counter-position. Two interpretations may both remain incomplete while one survives relevant objections that collapse the other. Two options may both involve cost while only one remains structurally available. Two models may both be bounded while one better distinguishes the cases for which the comparison was declared.

Revision-possibility therefore does not erase comparative structure. Formally:

\[
\boxed{
\text{No final truth}
\not\Rightarrow
\text{all relevant readings are equivalent.}
}
\]

This does not license the inverse mistake. If one claim is better warranted for a particular question, it does not follow that it is superior in every respect, across every frame, for every role, or at every temporal position. Local superiority does not silently become universal superiority. Nor does one successful comparison establish a transitive order over an entire domain.

The distinction matters because incomparability is itself easily misread. If two objects cannot responsibly be ranked under a given VECTOR claim, this does not show that they are equal. It shows that no warranted dominance relation has been established under the relevant conditions. Equality, trade-off, incomparability, and non-capture are different results. A theory that collapses them into one undifferentiated refusal to rank loses information just as surely as a theory that ranks everything.

VECTOR therefore treats non-finality as a condition of revisability rather than as a doctrine of equivalence. Orientation remains bounded, corrigible, and open to loss. Yet within those bounds, some directional claims may be more strongly warranted than others.

This is the first sense in which direction must be earned. A claim does not become directional because it is familiar, recurring, normatively attractive, or embedded in PMS language. It becomes a candidate for direction when a relevant structural difference can be specified; it gains warrant only to the extent that the relation remains coherent under the transformations and counterpressures appropriate to the claim.

## 1.3 The Open Discriminative Adequacy Problem in PMS

PMS Base leaves a particularly important question unresolved. Its own methodological framing distinguishes generative productivity from completeness and explicitly keeps open whether PMS is more discriminating than rival grammars or merely more assimilative across heterogeneous material. This is not a marginal limitation. It concerns the difference between a grammar that can translate many structures into a common vocabulary and a grammar that can preserve the differences that matter among them.

A highly expressive framework can fail precisely because it succeeds too easily. If almost any resistant material can be redescribed within the grammar, translation success may cease to discriminate between genuine explanatory purchase and mere assimilative capacity. More structure can produce more legibility without producing more warrant. Coherence can grow faster than correction.

The question of discriminative adequacy is therefore distinct from both completeness and empirical validation. A theory may be incomplete but discriminating. It may be broad but poorly discriminating. It may be internally coherent while failing to react appropriately to counterexamples. It may translate rival descriptions while leaving unresolved whether translation preserved or erased the relevant difference.

PMS has already developed several internal disciplines against these risks: explicit boundedness, calibration, rival sensitivity, non-capture, claim ceilings, correction over coherence, stop conditions, and self-critique under load. Yet these disciplines do not by themselves answer the directional question. They constrain how claims may be made and revised; they do not establish what relation, if any, exists among the local directions that repeatedly appear across PMS.

VECTOR takes up this narrower open problem. It does not claim to solve the completeness of PMS, establish its comparative superiority over all rivals, or provide external validation. It asks instead whether a bounded theory of structural orientation can make directional discrimination itself explicit and testable.

This distinction is crucial. If VECTOR succeeds, it does not prove PMS true. It provides a vocabulary and test architecture for claims such as: this reconstruction is locally more adequate for the declared question; this directional relation collapses under role rotation; these two configurations remain incomparable; this apparent option is not effectively available; this convergence is merely genealogical repetition; this practical necessity exists only relative to a maintained constraint. Such claims remain open to external evidence, rival models, calibration failure, and genuine non-capture.

VECTOR therefore inherits the discriminative problem, not the authority to settle it in advance.

## 1.4 Distributed Direction in the PMS Ecosystem

The motivation for VECTOR does not arise from a lack of direction in PMS. It arises from direction being present in distributed, locally typed forms without yet being reconstructed as a general theory of orientation.

PMS Base itself contains directional structure: impulse introduces directed activation within praxis, asymmetry differentiates burden and exposure, temporality changes what remains available, distance alters reflective room, integration distinguishes coherent consolidation from unresolved structure, and self-binding changes the trajectory of later praxis. These functions are not equivalent to VECTOR’s later DIR (Direction), ROT (Rotation), or DIST (Distance); they are part of the operator grammar from which local directional relations can become legible.

The domain and method papers make the distributed character clearer. EDEN distinguishes truthful coordination of real asymmetry from false coordination that preserves the appearance of reciprocity while displacing its actual structure. CRITIQUE distinguishes reaction from correction-capable follow-up and treats publicness, distance, and timing as structurally consequential. DISCIPLINE prefers bounded, correctable use over unrestricted coherence and explicitly allows reduction, suspension, or refusal. CONFLICT shows that not every mature structural tension can be reintegrated and that continuity requirements may survive where symmetry does not. ANTICIPATION shows that confirmation does not erase the need for timing discipline and that effective capacity, rather than abstract possibility, matters for responsibility. UNDER LOAD turns PMS’s own success conditions into pressure conditions and insists that productivity, portability, implementation, and self-application do not become warrants merely by accumulating.

These are not instances of one already-proven common direction. They differ in object, function, frame, and burden. Their recurrence therefore cannot be counted as independent evidence for a meta-orientation. Shared terminology, shared operators, shared genealogy, or shared authorship can produce repetition without convergence.

The relevant starting point is weaker:

\[
\boxed{
\text{PMS contains distributed local direction.}
}
\]

Whether those local directions support a bounded common orientation remains an open claim. A recurring preference becomes a VECTOR candidate, not a VECTOR result.

This distinction prevents VECTOR from becoming circular. It would be trivial to inspect PMS, collect the directions PMS already favors, name their recurrence “VECTOR,” and return the result as confirmation that PMS was oriented correctly. That would add vocabulary, not discrimination. A genuine meta-orientation must permit the local claims to diverge, trade off, collapse under rotation, lose support under rival pressure, or fail to produce any robust resultant at all.

VECTOR therefore approaches distributed direction as material to be tested, not as authority to be inherited.

## 1.5 The Problem of Unacknowledged Direction

A framework can avoid explicit ranking while still carrying implicit direction. Indeed, the more disciplined and portable a framework becomes, the easier it is for its recurring preferences to acquire practical force without ever being formally acknowledged as directional claims.

This creates two risks.

The first is hidden normativity. If a framework repeatedly treats correction, truthful coordination, reversibility, explicit asymmetry, boundedness, or non-capture as preferable structural outcomes but never states the conditions under which those preferences are warranted, the distinction between description and orientation becomes unstable. Direction then enters through vocabulary, examples, stopping rules, or application habits while remaining unavailable for direct criticism.

The second risk is the opposite: a defensive refusal of direction. Concern about moralism, authority, or universal ranking can become so strong that every structural distinction is rhetorically flattened into mere difference. Yet this would undermine the framework’s own discriminative ambition. If a correction-capable reconstruction and a demonstrably reference-breaking reconstruction must be treated as equivalent merely because no final standpoint exists, the theory has lost the ability to distinguish stronger from weaker claims within its own stated bounds.

Making direction explicit is therefore a form of constraint, not inflation. An explicit directional claim can be asked to declare its reference, frame, question, perspective, and temporal position. It can be rotated. It can be rivalized. Its critical regressions can be exposed. Its scope can be reduced. It can become trade-off, incomparable, suspended, or non-capturable. What remains tacit is harder to test.

This is why VECTOR does not begin by declaring what the direction of PMS is. It begins by constructing conditions under which directional claims can lose.

\[
\boxed{
\text{A direction that cannot survive admissible rotation is not yet robust orientation.}
}
\]

The same discipline applies to practical success. An orientation can have causal power without being true. A false belief can alter option visibility, stabilize expectations, delay correction, redistribute cost, or close an action corridor. Practical effect is therefore not evidence of orientation truth. A theory of orientation that ignored this distinction would risk validating the very claims that happen to organize behavior most successfully.

Explicit orientation must consequently remain answerable on two sides at once: it must be strong enough to discriminate and weak enough to be reduced by the conditions it claims to track.

## 1.6 Scope and Contribution of PMS–VECTOR

PMS–VECTOR proposes a late meta-reconstructive praxeological theory of orientation within the PMS ecosystem. It is late because its strongest warrant depends on the local structures it reconstructs already existing as separately bounded claims; meta-reconstructive because it examines relations among those reconstructed structures rather than adding a new ontological layer; praxeological because its object remains situated praxis under frame, asymmetry, temporality, possibility, consequence, and binding; and orientational because its central question concerns bounded direction rather than prescription.

Its minimal architecture is:

\[
\boxed{
VECTOR:=\langle DIR,ROT\rangle,
\qquad
V_t(O\mid F,Q,\pi)=\langle DIR_t,RP_t\rangle.
}
\]

DIR (Direction) reconstructs bounded structural direction under declared comparison conditions. ROT (Rotation) makes perspective an explicit variable and tests whether directional claims survive admissible changes in viewing position while preserving the relevant reference; it can also generate new relational visibility without thereby increasing same-claim robustness. Effective orientation–enactment separation remains analytically important, but the paper's own reduction test retains DIST (Distance) only as a derived supporting relation rather than a third core component.

From this core and its supporting praxis relations, the paper develops four further consequences. First, orientation is connected to a typed praxeological option field in which conceivable, visible, available, actionable, and viable alternatives must not be conflated. Second, agency is treated not only as selection among given alternatives but, where effective capacity exists, as the bounded ability to change option availability or relations among options. Third, orientation is made consequence-sensitive through a non-fungible topology of action, non-action, window, binding, reversal, externalized, relational, and downstream costs. Fourth, orientation is subjected to convergence, rival, counter-scene, provenance, and self-reduction tests before stronger practical claims are permitted.

The resulting theory makes a limited but non-trivial claim about practical attribution while preserving the normative firewall. VECTOR does not derive categorical moral prescriptions from structural description. It distinguishes choice commands from orientation-mediated structural attributability and from constraint-relative practical necessity. Where VECTOR claims that **orientation itself** changes attribution, that move additionally requires the orientation to be externally supported where needed, actor-accessible, sufficiently understood for contest or response, praxis-available inside the relevant window, and connected to effective capacity and consequence. Maintained constraints may then narrow a practical response class, but none of this increases authority, establishes the legitimacy of the operative constraint, or converts practical necessity into moral goodness.

VECTOR therefore does not modify PMS Base, function as a validator, provide a universal scale, or establish a final theory of better praxis. Its intended contribution is narrower:

1. to make local structural direction explicit without presuming total order;
2. to make perspective a testable variable rather than an unacknowledged privilege;
3. to connect orientation to reversibility, real and illusory possibility, second-order agency, consequence, and timing without collapsing them into a single metric;
4. to distinguish recurrence from convergent orientation and robustness from self-confirmation;
5. to reconstruct how sufficiently warranted and actor-relative uptake of orientation can change later praxis conditions without acquiring moral or decision authority; and
6. to remain reducible, including to the result that no robust VECTOR relation is available for a case, domain, or sufficiently broad claim.

The current paper already applies this reduction discipline to itself. DIST (Distance) loses core-component status; the “Adult Orientation” construct reduces to supporting shorthand because its deletion causes no claim-critical discriminative loss; and a same-burden non-PMS MCDA narrows the primary scope of DIR (Direction), ROT (Rotation), and standalone VECTOR where a commensurable orientation problem is externally calibrated and the rival carries the directional and sensitivity burden more directly.

The central wager is therefore not that praxis possesses a single hidden direction. It is that non-finality can coexist with warranted structural orientation, provided direction remains bounded, perspectivally vulnerable, consequence-sensitive, revisable, and genuinely capable of non-capture.

The paper begins from this wager and then turns immediately to its first condition: why a theory of meta-orientation must come after, rather than before, the local structures whose relations it seeks to reconstruct.

---

# Part I — Position and Formal Ground

# 2. Why VECTOR Comes Late

VECTOR is a late construction in the PMS ecosystem, but lateness here is neither chronological prestige nor theoretical elevation. It does not mean that VECTOR occupies a higher level from which earlier PMS work can finally be judged. It does not mean that the preceding papers were incomplete drafts waiting for a meta-theory to reveal what they were really doing. Nor does it mean that PMS has developed toward VECTOR as toward a necessary endpoint.

The claim is narrower. VECTOR's strongest warrant depends on being posterior to the local structures it seeks to relate. Imposed in advance, its direction would be assumed rather than earned.

This distinction matters because a theory of orientation is unusually vulnerable to circularity. If a general directional principle is declared first and local cases are subsequently interpreted through it, the apparent recurrence of that direction may reveal little more than the prior imposition of the principle. A framework can then mistake consistency with its own starting assumptions for evidence that the assumptions were correct. VECTOR is designed against that move. It begins only after a sufficiently differentiated field of local PMS claims already exists and asks whether relations among those claims support a bounded meta-orientation that remains vulnerable to reduction.

The relevant sequence is therefore epistemic before it is historical:

\[
\text{praxis}
\rightarrow
\text{local differentiation}
\rightarrow
\text{structured reconstruction}
\rightarrow
\text{distributed directional pressure}
\rightarrow
\text{meta-orientation candidate}.
\]

The sequence does not prove VECTOR. It defines the burden under which VECTOR may responsibly appear.

PMS Base itself is explicit about the corresponding limit. Its generative reach does not establish completeness, optimality, discriminative adequacy, or comparative superiority. The fact that a wide range of praxis structures can be translated into a shared operator grammar demonstrates methodological productivity; it does not demonstrate that the grammar has captured every relevant distinction or that its own recurrent preferences have earned priority over rival accounts. PMS therefore leaves open exactly the kind of question that VECTOR takes up: not whether structure can be rendered legible, but when a structured difference can carry warranted direction.

The late position of VECTOR should accordingly be understood as a condition of accountability. The more material already exists before the meta-claim is introduced, the less freedom VECTOR has to define its own evidence retroactively. Local claims can resist convergence. They can preserve incompatible constraints. They can expose limits that a later synthesis would prefer to smooth away. They can show that an apparent ecosystem-wide direction was produced by shared genealogy rather than distinct local burdens. Lateness, in this sense, is not a claim to maturity. It is an attempt to reduce the degree to which the meta-theory can author the material from which it later claims support.

## 2.1 Bottom-Up Before Meta

PMS was not built by first positing a universal scale of better praxis and then assigning observed cases to positions on that scale. Its characteristic movement has instead been reconstructive and bottom-up. Concrete praxis problems generated recurring distinctions; those distinctions were formalized into an operator grammar; domain papers exposed the grammar to different pressures; application discipline constrained the passage from readability to use; self-critique turned the framework's own strengths into testing conditions; and STRATA later made controlled analytical transformation explicit without converting analytical mobility into additional authority.

VECTOR inherits the logic of this development without treating the development itself as evidence for VECTOR.

The distinction is crucial. A historical sequence can make a later question intelligible without making its answer true. That PMS repeatedly encountered asymmetry, temporality, correction, reversibility, responsibility, non-capture, and the danger of authority inflation does not show that these structures converge on one hidden axis. It shows only that a sufficiently rich field of locally typed directional pressure now exists for a meta-orientation problem to be posed with more precision than would have been possible at the beginning.

The relevant movement can be stated schematically:

\[
\text{praxis}
\rightarrow
\text{local distinctions}
\rightarrow
\text{operator grammar}
\rightarrow
\text{domain pressure}
\rightarrow
\text{application discipline}
\rightarrow
\text{self-pressure}
\rightarrow
\text{controlled transformation}
\rightarrow
\text{orientation reconstruction}.
\]

This is not a dependency chain in which every later element validates the earlier ones. It is a record of increasing differentiation. Each stage adds constraints that the later meta-claim must preserve rather than erase.

The bottom-up character of PMS matters for three reasons.

First, it prevents VECTOR from starting with an abstract quality axis. The theory does not begin with a function such as

\[
GoodPraxis(X)>GoodPraxis(Y)
\]

and ask how observed structures can be arranged beneath it. Such a construction would import the very ordering problem that VECTOR is supposed to test. Instead, VECTOR begins from already bounded local claims whose directional content is heterogeneous. One paper may discriminate between reaction and correction. Another may distinguish truthful from false coordination. Another may show that an apparently available option is not effectively actionable for the relevant role. Another may conclude that a conflict remains mature but non-integrable. None of these claims is yet a universal rank.

Second, bottom-up construction preserves the possibility that local differences remain irreducibly local. A later meta-theory has no entitlement to convert every recurring distinction into a shared direction merely because the distinctions occur inside the same ecosystem. Some may turn out to be structurally homologous. Some may only appear similar because they share vocabulary. Some may support conditional convergence. Others may remain trade-offs or fail to enter a common comparison at all. The local claims therefore precede VECTOR not merely as source material but as constraints on what VECTOR may legitimately say.

Third, bottom-up posteriority places a burden on the theory to distinguish reconstruction from retrospective teleology. Once a meta-pattern becomes visible, it is tempting to reread the entire prior development as if the pattern had always been its destination. VECTOR rejects that narrative. The fact that earlier PMS work can now be related through a later concept does not mean the concept was latent as a completed theory in every earlier paper. Nor does it mean that the earlier papers gain additional validity from becoming readable through VECTOR.

A later reconstruction may be illuminating without being historically necessary.

\[
\boxed{
\text{Meta-orientation is posterior to local praxeological differentiation.}
}
\]

Posteriority is therefore neither reverence for chronology nor a claim that theory must always develop in this order. It is a specific epistemic advantage in the present case: VECTOR can be constrained by structures it did not itself invent for the purpose of supporting VECTOR.

That advantage is limited. Shared authorship, shared PMS vocabulary, shared operator commitments, and continued dialogic development mean that the local sources are not independent experiments. Part IV will therefore refuse to count mere recurrence as convergence. At this stage, the weaker point is enough: the local field exists before the meta-claim, and that makes it possible for the meta-claim to encounter resistance rather than merely reproduce its own premise.

> **Posteriority reduces one form of retroactive imposition; it does not create epistemic independence.**

The sentence is a boundary on the lateness claim itself. VECTOR may gain a better opportunity to encounter resistance from prior local structures; it does not thereby gain independent evidence, escape shared genealogy, or convert chronological sequence into epistemic rank.

## 2.2 From Local Structure to Meta-Orientation

The object introduced by VECTOR is not simply another domain of praxis. It is a relation among already reconstructed relations.

Let

\[
d_1,d_2,\ldots,d_n
\]

denote locally typed directional relations. The notation is intentionally minimal. At this stage, the claims may concern correction, coordination, bounded adequacy, responsibility, reversibility, transformation, or another explicitly declared question. They need not share the same object, frame, or immediate purpose. Their common feature is only that a relevant structural difference appears to carry local direction rather than mere distinction.

VECTOR asks:

\[
\boxed{
\text{What relation, if any, exists among these directional relations?}
}
\]

The phrase *if any* is constitutive. Meta-orientation is not the automatic aggregation of local direction. The presence of several directional claims does not imply a resultant:

\[
\boxed{
\{d_1,\ldots,d_n\}
\not\Rightarrow
DIR_R.
}
\]

The new level of analysis is therefore relationally second-order. It takes already bounded reconstructions as objects of further comparison without granting the new comparison superior authority.

\[
\boxed{
\text{Meta does not mean superior. Meta means relationally second-order.}
}
\]

This formulation protects VECTOR from a common ambiguity in multi-layer theoretical systems. A meta-level can easily be read as a governing level: if it speaks *about* several lower-level claims, it appears to possess a broader view and therefore a stronger right to decide among them. Nothing in VECTOR warrants that inference. Broader scope increases burden. It does not inherit authority.

The distinction is particularly important inside PMS because its ecosystem contains several different claim types. Base specifies a proposed generative grammar. Domain papers apply and stress that grammar within bounded problems. DISCIPLINE governs the responsible use of PMS-based readings. UNDER LOAD turns PMS's own strengths into pressure conditions. STRATA specifies controlled analytical transformations. These are not simply rungs on one ladder. Their functions differ. Their claim ceilings differ. Their failures differ.

VECTOR enters this differentiated environment not by placing itself above the existing claim types but by posing a new question about relations among directional structures that have emerged across them. The answer may reveal a common direction, but the question alone creates no hierarchy.

This matters for the meaning of the later word *resultant*. When Part IV eventually introduces a resultant orientation, it will not denote a vector sum, a majority outcome, or a ruling by the widest layer. It will denote a new bounded claim that must preserve the differences and constraints from which it is reconstructed. Nothing in Chapter 2 allows that claim to be presumed.

The distinction also clarifies why VECTOR cannot be treated as a simple summary of PMS preferences. A summary asks what themes recur. A meta-orientation asks whether recurrence survives explicit burdens of provenance, rotation, rival sensitivity, non-compensation, and possible collapse. The former can be produced by reading. The latter requires a theory of loss.

The second-order status of VECTOR is therefore positive but narrow. It gives the theory a legitimate new object: relations among directional relations. It does not grant an unrestricted new perspective from which the rest of PMS becomes subordinate.

## 2.3 Earned Rather Than Imposed Direction

A theory of direction is only informative if direction can fail.

This requirement becomes especially important in a mature framework. PMS already contains recurring preferences in the weak sense that some structures repeatedly receive more favorable treatment than others: correction over uncorrectable reaction, explicit asymmetry over false symmetry, bounded claims over uncontrolled expansion, real capacity over merely imagined possibility, reference-preserving transformation over type drift, and non-capture over forced assimilation where the framework does not responsibly hold. Such recurrences are theoretically interesting. They are not yet VECTOR results.

The circular form to avoid is straightforward:

\[
PMS\ prefers\ X
\Rightarrow
VECTOR(X)
\Rightarrow
PMS\ was\ right\ to\ prefer\ X.
\]

If VECTOR merely names the pattern already built into the source material and then returns that pattern as evidence of orientation, the theory has added a label rather than a discriminative burden.

The correct initial status of recurrence is weaker:

\[
\boxed{
\text{A recurring preference becomes a VECTOR candidate, not a VECTOR result.}
}
\]

To call direction *earned* therefore does not moralize the claim. It means that the directional relation must be exposed to conditions under which it could be weakened, reversed, split, narrowed, suspended, or refused. A direction that is protected against these losses has been installed rather than reconstructed.

The full burden is distributed across the rest of the paper. Part II makes perspective an explicit test variable through ROT (Rotation). Part III makes option status, agency, cost, and timing available as sources of correction. Part IV distinguishes recurrence from meaningful convergence and develops bounded test-burden closure. Part VI asks whether VECTOR itself deserves standalone status or should be reduced.

Chapter 2 requires only the governing principle:

\[
\boxed{
\text{Direction must remain genuinely reducible.}
}
\]

Reduction can take several forms. A broad claim may become local. An apparent dominance relation may become trade-off. A comparison may become incomparable. An initially plausible meta-direction may dissolve when shared genealogy is exposed. A claim may survive only under one perspective and therefore lose generality. A theory component may later prove redundant with existing PMS structures. These are not external embarrassments to be hidden after the fact. They are conditions under which *earned rather than imposed* acquires operational meaning.

The rule also blocks a subtle use of authorial or ecosystem continuity as evidence. A framework developed over time by a coherent authorial process will naturally contain recurrent commitments. Those commitments can reflect learning, refinement, and genuine structural convergence. They can also reflect stable priors. VECTOR therefore treats continuity as material for analysis, not as an epistemic multiplier.

The strongest formulation appropriate here is accordingly modest:

> VECTOR investigates whether distributed local direction can support bounded meta-orientation.

It does not yet claim that such an orientation exists.

## 2.4 Why VECTOR Is Not PMS Base

VECTOR does not extend the PMS operator grammar.

PMS Base remains the proposed generative structure built from the ordered operator set

\[
\Delta \rightarrow \nabla \rightarrow \Box \rightarrow \Lambda \rightarrow Α \rightarrow \Omega \rightarrow \Theta \rightarrow \Phi \rightarrow Χ \rightarrow \Sigma \rightarrow \Psi.
\]

VECTOR neither inserts an additional operator into this sequence nor claims to reveal an operator that Base had failed to include. Its relation to PMS Base begins from an open problem explicitly retained by Base itself. The Base paper presents PMS as productive without treating productivity as closure. Questions of completeness, optimal ordering, discriminative adequacy, and comparative superiority remain open. Translation into PMS terms can demonstrate formal tractability without demonstrating exhaustive capture. Genuine non-capture remains methodologically possible.

VECTOR takes up one consequence of that openness. If PMS can render structural differences legible without settling comparative superiority in advance, a further theory may ask when some of those differences support bounded direction. But the further question does not modify the grammar that made the differences legible.

The resulting type boundaries must be explicit:

\[
\boxed{DIR\neq\nabla}
\]

\[
\boxed{ROT\neq\Phi}
\]

\[
\boxed{DIST\neq Χ}
\]

and, for the temporal index introduced later,

\[
\boxed{t\neq\Theta.}
\]

These inequalities are not claims that the concepts are unrelated. On the contrary, VECTOR depends on PMS structures for much of what it later reconstructs. The point is that relation is not type identity.

### DIR and directed activation

The PMS operator \(\nabla\) (directed activation) concerns impulse or directed activation within the generative grammar of praxis. DIR (Direction) does not. DIR is a VECTOR-side relation that states, under declared comparison conditions, that a relevant structural difference carries direction. A praxis configuration may contain strong \(\nabla\) while no valid DIR comparison exists between it and another configuration. Conversely, a DIR claim may compare two reconstructions whose relevant difference does not concern impulse at all.

Treating DIR (Direction) as a renamed \(\nabla\) (directed activation) would therefore collapse levels of claim. One is a Base operator participating in the generation of praxis structure; the other is a later comparative relation among typed structures.

### ROT and recontextualization

The distinction between ROT (Rotation) and \(\Phi\) (recontextualization) is equally important. Recontextualization is part of PMS Base. ROT is a VECTOR operation on the conditions under which an orientation claim is examined. A ROT may alter role position, affected-role position, inside/outside standpoint, temporal cut, asymmetry position, counterfactual condition, rival model, or granularity. Some of these moves may involve recontextualization in a broad sense. That does not make ROT a Base operator.

ROT (Rotation) is defined by its function in orientation: it makes the viewing condition explicit and asks what happens to a directional claim under admissible change. Its validity depends on reference continuity, declared change, held-constant conditions, question relevance, and genuine claim-loss capacity. Those are VECTOR conditions, not a redefinition of \(\Phi\) (recontextualization).

### DIST and distance

Likewise, PMS already contains \(Χ\) (distance). VECTOR does not add distance to a framework that lacked it. DIST (Distance) is retained only as derived shorthand for *effective orientation–enactment separation* at a given point in praxis. \(Χ\) can contribute strongly to that relation, while \(Θ\) (temporality), \(Ψ\) (self-binding), \(Ω\) (asymmetry), \(Α\) (attractor), publicness, option status, capacity, and reversibility can change how much practical correction room remains. Chapter 19 shows that this relation is useful but does not earn an independent core component. DIST therefore summarizes a relation; it does not replace or outrank the source structures that generate it.

### Temporal index and temporality

The same distinction applies to \(t\) and \(\Theta\) (temporality). VECTOR requires a temporal index because a claim evaluated at \(t_0\) may not be the same claim at \(t_1\). Evidence, options, capacity, bindings, costs, and available corrections may change. The index records when a VECTOR state is being evaluated. It is not a second symbol for PMS temporality.

These distinctions allow VECTOR to remain grounded in PMS without presenting itself as a repair to PMS Base. The positive relation is therefore best stated as follows:

> VECTOR takes up an open discriminative problem generated around PMS without modifying the grammar through which PMS makes praxis legible.

That relation is stronger than mere thematic continuity and weaker than Base extension. It is the appropriate position for a late meta-reconstructive claim.

## 2.5 Why VECTOR Is Not PMS-DISCIPLINE

PMS-DISCIPLINE begins from a different problem.

Its concern is not primarily whether a structural relation carries direction. It governs the passage from PMS readability to responsible PMS use. In the language of DISCIPLINE, a PMS reading may be structurally possible and still not be licensed for the requested output, use, audience, or degree of person-nearness. A template can increase inspectability without increasing authority. A completed workflow can preserve accountability without becoming proof. A reading can be bounded, downgraded, suspended, refused, or redirected even where PMS remains structurally productive.

That function must remain distinct from VECTOR.

The contrast can be stated compactly:

\[
\boxed{
\text{DISCIPLINE: May this claim or application carry this use?}
}
\]

\[
\boxed{
\text{VECTOR: What directional relation, if any, is warranted among the relevant structures?}
}
\]

The first is a use-boundary question. The second is an orientation question.

This distinction has practical consequences. Suppose VECTOR supports a bounded directional claim such that

\[
X\prec_VY.
\]

Nothing in that relation implies that \(Y\) may be used more broadly, asserted more publicly, applied more closely to persons, or granted a higher claim ceiling. A structurally stronger orientation claim may still be inappropriate for a requested use. Conversely, DISCIPLINE may permit a bounded PMS application even where VECTOR cannot establish any dominance relation among competing structures.

Thus:

\[
\boxed{
X\prec_VY
\not\Rightarrow
\text{higher application permission for }Y.
}
\]

The separation prevents two forms of authority borrowing.

The first would be for VECTOR to borrow DISCIPLINE's practical gatekeeping force and present a directional relation as permission to act. The second would be for DISCIPLINE to borrow VECTOR's later orientation vocabulary and present an application decision as evidence that one structure was theoretically superior. Neither move is licensed.

DISCIPLINE nevertheless provides important local precedent for VECTOR. Its insistence on claim ceilings, bounded adequacy, rival pressure, correction over coherence, suspension, stop-capability, and non-inheritance demonstrates that a framework can become more useful by constraining what a claim may carry rather than by maximizing the amount of structure applied. It also provides an important precursor to the later VECTOR distinction between practical effect and truth: a false or insufficiently warranted orientation can still acquire practical power when embedded in use, workflow, publicness, or institutional process.

But precedent is not authority. VECTOR does not become valid because DISCIPLINE already values boundedness. DISCIPLINE does not become a VECTOR validator because its local use-logic resembles later VECTOR constraints.

The relationship is therefore deliberately asymmetric:

> DISCIPLINE can constrain the use of a VECTOR-informed claim, while VECTOR does not determine whether that use is permitted merely by making the claim more directional or more robust.

This separation will matter increasingly as the paper approaches practical necessity. More orientation can make a claim more answerable without making its holder more entitled.

## 2.6 Why VECTOR Is Not PMS-STRATA

The boundary with PMS-STRATA requires more care because the two projects share several methodological commitments and because ROT can superficially resemble controlled analytical transformation.

STRATA specifies how PMS structures may be composed, decomposed, and functionally projected across declared granularities and relative levels while preserving source traceability, origin-type integrity, transformation loss, claim boundaries, stop, and non-capture. Its three explicit operations are

\[
COMPOSE,
\qquad
DECOMPOSE,
\qquad
PROJECT\_AS.
\]

STRATA is explicit that these operations do not form a superior PMS layer, do not add new Base operators, do not establish an ontology of levels, and do not generate additional application authority merely by allowing more analytical movement. Transformation itself creates no meta-authority.

VECTOR borrows heavily from this discipline. In particular, it shares the need for declared reference, explicit frame, bounded claim type, reference continuity, non-compensation, visible loss, stop conditions, and legitimate non-capture. These are exactly the kinds of constraints a theory of rotation requires if perspective change is not to become arbitrary reframing.

Yet shared discipline does not imply shared operation.

\[
\boxed{
\text{Borrowed discipline is not inherited authority.}
}
\]

Nor is borrowed discipline identical procedure.

### ROT is not PROJECT_AS

The central distinction is:

\[
\boxed{ROT\neq PROJECT\_AS.}
\]

PROJECT_AS retains the origin type of an analytical object while assigning it a bounded contextual target function within a declared projection. The operation is therefore a controlled transformation of analytical position and function. ROT (Rotation) has a different primary purpose. It changes a declared viewing condition in order to test or extend an orientation relation.

A simple role rotation illustrates the difference. Suppose the same praxis configuration is examined first from the position of an acting role and then from the position of an affected role:

\[
\pi_A\rightarrow\pi_B.
\]

If the reference object, frame, question, and relevant temporal position remain sufficiently stable, the operation can test whether the same DIR (Direction) claim survives the changed relational position. Nothing in this move requires the referent to acquire a new target function. The analyst is not necessarily projecting the source object into a new analytical type. The operation is designed to expose perspective dependence in an orientation claim.

Some ROT (Rotation) families will involve more substantial changes. A rival-model rotation or a granularity rotation can approach the boundary where a new bounded claim is being produced rather than the same claim tested. This is precisely why VECTOR later distinguishes robustness-bearing rotation from exploratory probe. STRATA's transformation discipline helps VECTOR police that boundary, but it does not make the operations identical.

### Reference continuity is shared, not owned

Reference continuity is therefore a methodological point of contact. STRATA requires the historical or analytical referent to remain identifiable through transformation. VECTOR requires sufficient continuity to know whether a rotation still bears on the original orientation claim.

The fact that both frameworks need continuity says something important about disciplined reconstruction: analytical freedom becomes unreliable when the referent can change silently while the claim is treated as stable. But this common problem does not establish a common operation type.

A useful rule is:

> Reference continuity constrains both STRATA and VECTOR, but it does not make their operations identical.

### Analytical transformation and praxis transformation

A second possible confusion appears later in the paper. Part III will argue that actors can sometimes alter the option field itself: they can create options, open blocked options, preserve or close possibilities, change sequence, or transform relations among options. This claim must not be absorbed into STRATA as a fourth operation.

STRATA transforms analytical PMS structures. Option-space transformation concerns real changes in praxis conditions where effective capacity exists.

\[
\boxed{
\text{analytic transformation}
\neq
\text{praxis transformation}.
}
\]

and therefore:

\[
\boxed{
\text{Option-space transformation is not a fourth STRATA operation.}
}
\]

The distinction is not merely terminological. If analytical operations and real agency were collapsed, the theory could easily confuse a newly imaginable reconstruction with a newly available practical option. Part III will treat that confusion as option illusion.

### Non-compensation and no global rank

STRATA also provides an important precedent for a later VECTOR principle: stronger performance on one dimension does not automatically compensate for claim-critical loss elsewhere. A transformation can gain resolution while losing source continuity; a finer reconstruction can become less praxeologically useful; a projection can become elegant while exceeding its evidence. Similarly, VECTOR will refuse to aggregate all local strengths into a global score.

Again, however, the relation is methodological lineage rather than inherited warrant. STRATA shows that controlled analytical mobility can remain bounded and non-authoritative. VECTOR uses that discipline to build a different operation type around orientation.

The clean relation is therefore:

\[
\boxed{
\text{STRATA supplies transformation discipline;
VECTOR supplies no additional STRATA operation.}
}
\]

## 2.7 Why VECTOR Is Not PMS UNDER LOAD II

UNDER LOAD is perhaps the easiest PMS source to misread as a precursor that VECTOR now resolves. That reading would undermine both projects.

UNDER LOAD begins from an intentionally demanding posture: assume PMS works strongly enough that obvious failure is no longer the only interesting test. The central concern is whether the conditions that make PMS productive—coherence, portability, translation, application discipline, workflow tooling, transformation structure, self-application, and inspectability—can themselves reduce discriminative force. A strong grammar may become difficult to challenge not because it has become final, but because it can continue producing coherent readings under an ever wider range of conditions.

The relevant principle is severe:

> PMS success is a pressure condition, not external warrant.

VECTOR does not supersede this pressure. It intensifies its relevance.

A theory that explicitly reconstructs direction is especially vulnerable to the risks UNDER LOAD identifies. Direction can become hidden normativity. Rotational robustness can become self-confirmation. A broad resultant can become ecosystem authority. A self-test can become final self-arbitration. A formal record can become validation theater. A carefully bounded framework can still become more difficult to resist simply because it has developed increasingly sophisticated ways to absorb objection.

The relation is therefore:

\[
\boxed{
UNDER\ LOAD
\rightarrow
VECTOR\ pressure
}
\]

not:

\[
\boxed{
UNDER\ LOAD
\rightarrow
VECTOR\ warrant.
}
\]

VECTOR comes after UNDER LOAD only in the weak sense that it is developed in an ecosystem where self-pressure has already become explicit. It does not come after it in the sense of having answered or retired its critique.

Indeed, VECTOR must remain one of the structures placed under load. Its own success would create exactly the kind of risk that UNDER LOAD treats as theoretically significant. If DIR (Direction) becomes useful across many domains, that portability cannot become proof of universal direction. If ROT (Rotation) generates elegant self-corrections, those corrections cannot become evidence that VECTOR is immune to external critique. If the framework can explain every counterexample as a trade-off, scope issue, or non-capture case, its apparent flexibility may itself require pressure. If a reader learns to see everything through DIR–ROT, that learned legibility cannot be granted priority merely because it has become fluent.

The strongest relation between the papers can therefore be stated directly:

> VECTOR does not come after UNDER LOAD in the sense of superseding it; it comes after it in the sense of remaining answerable to it.

This is more than a disclaimer. Part IV will require self-application without final self-arbitration. Part VI will specify failure families and reduction conditions under which VECTOR itself must become smaller. Those are not optional gestures of humility. They are structural consequences of developing a theory inside an ecosystem that already recognizes successful self-stabilization as a possible failure mode.

## 2.8 Claim Type and Ecosystem Position

The preceding boundaries allow VECTOR to be stated positively.

PMS–VECTOR proposes a new claim type for its own work:

\[
\boxed{
\text{late meta-reconstructive praxeological orientation claim}.
}
\]

At paper level, PMS–VECTOR is a late meta-reconstructive praxeological orientation paper.

Each term carries a specific burden.

### Late

*Late* means posterior to the relevant local structures whose relations VECTOR investigates. It does not mean final, higher, more mature, or historically necessary. The local field has to exist before the meta-relation can be meaningfully tested, because otherwise the meta-theory could simply define the material that later appears to support it.

### Meta-reconstructive

*Meta-reconstructive* means that VECTOR reconstructs relations among already reconstructed structures. It does not posit a new ontological layer above praxis. It does not convert analytical second-order status into authority. A meta-claim may have broader scope while remaining more vulnerable because broader scope increases the number of differences it must preserve.

### Praxeological

*Praxeological* means that VECTOR remains bound to situated praxis: frames, roles, asymmetry, temporality, distance, binding, possibility, consequence, and effective capacity. It does not become a free-standing metaphysics of value or a general ranking system for persons, cultures, institutions, or theories.

### Orientation

*Orientation* means that the primary problem concerns direction. It asks whether a structural difference supports a bounded relation such as local superiority, trade-off, incomparability, or non-capture under declared conditions. Orientation is not identical to prescription. A directional relation may later alter practical accountability without itself becoming a command.

The category itself should not be retroactively attributed to prior PMS work. The phrase *late meta-reconstructive praxeological orientation claim* is a VECTOR proposal. Earlier papers supplied local structures and pressures; they did not already contain this completed claim type under another name.

This distinction matters for provenance. Later, Appendix F will separate corpus-carried claims from VECTOR reconstructions, VECTOR-new claims, and provisional formalizations. The claim-type label belongs on the VECTOR side of that boundary.

Placed within the PMS ecosystem, VECTOR is therefore not PMS Base, not a domain lens, not an application discipline, not a workflow authority, not a transformation method, not a validator, and not a governance layer. These negatives do not define the paper by exclusion. They prevent its broader relational scope from borrowing the authority of claim types it does not possess.

The positive position is enough:

> VECTOR is a late attempt to reconstruct, test, and reduce bounded directional relations among already structured forms of praxis.

## 2.9 More Orientation Does Not Mean More Authority

The final boundary of Chapter 2 concerns the practical temptation created by better orientation.

A claim that becomes more discriminating, more robust under rotation, more sensitive to consequence, or more explicit about option structure may deserve increased confidence within its declared scope. That increase does not change the holder's institutional role, moral entitlement, or authority over other actors.

Formally:

\[
\boxed{
Orientation\uparrow
\not\Rightarrow
Authority\uparrow.
}
\]

The distinction is easy to state and difficult to preserve. Better orientation often feels action-guiding. Once one position appears more robust than another, the step from *better warranted* to *therefore entitled to decide* can become psychologically and institutionally attractive. In collaborative settings, epistemic confidence can become role expansion. In public settings, legibility can become pressure. In governance settings, a structural claim can be mistaken for a mandate. In interpersonal settings, improved understanding can become an asserted right to bind others.

VECTOR rejects this conversion.

Authority may depend on law, consent, role, institution, expertise, mandate, moral premises, or other external conditions. VECTOR does not supply those conditions merely by making a structural relation more legible. A robust orientation may make the holder more answerable for what the orientation now reveals. It does not make the holder more entitled to impose the result.

A useful formulation is:

> **A better-oriented claim may become more answerable without making its holder more entitled.**

This asymmetry prepares the later practical-attribution argument. Part V will distinguish baseline PMS-LOGIC attributability from the narrower claim that a **new VECTOR orientation** changes attribution. The latter requires the orientation to become actor-accessible, sufficiently understood for contest or response, praxis-available in the relevant window, and connected to effective capacity and consequence. Where that bridge carries, structural attributability may change without creating general decision authority.

The two directions must remain separate throughout the paper:

\[
\text{orientation}
\rightarrow
\text{possible increased answerability}
\]

without:

\[
\text{orientation}
\rightarrow
\text{automatic entitlement}.
\]

VECTOR therefore enters the PMS ecosystem not as a higher court but as a bounded attempt to make distributed direction explicit enough to be compared, rotated, reduced, or refused.

That claim-type discipline now permits the formal object itself to be defined.

---

# 3. The VECTOR Object

A theory of orientation becomes unreliable if the object of orientation remains implicit. Statements such as *X has a higher VECTOR than Y*, *this position is more oriented*, or *that direction survives rotation* are not yet well-formed unless the relevant reference, frame, question, perspective, and temporal position are known.

VECTOR therefore begins its formal ground with a simple rule:

\[
\boxed{
\text{There is no context-free VECTOR state.}
}
\]

This rule does not mean that every analysis requires exhaustive contextual description. It means that a VECTOR claim is constituted by certain claim-bearing coordinates. Remove or silently change them and the claim may no longer be the same claim.

The minimal coordinates are:

\[
(O,F,Q,\pi,t),
\]

where \(O\) is the reference object, \(F\) the declared frame, \(Q\) the question and claim scope, \(\pi\) the perspectival position, and \(t\) the temporal position or evaluation index.

These variables are not decorative metadata. They establish what is being compared, under what boundary, for what question, from what relational position, and at what point in a changing praxis trajectory. DIR (Direction) and ROT (Rotation) acquire determinate meaning only after this ground is declared. Derived relations such as DIST (Distance) are likewise claim-bound to these coordinates rather than context-free.

The chapter therefore defines the smallest sufficiently typed object on which the later theory can operate. It does not yet define the detailed semantics of DIR (Direction), the ROT (Rotation) families, the Reversibility Gradient, or VECTOR dynamics. Those belong to Part II. The purpose here is prior: to make it impossible for orientation claims to float free of the conditions that give them meaning.

## 3.1 Reference Object

The first coordinate is the reference object:

\[
O=\text{reference object}.
\]

The term *object* is deliberately broad. VECTOR can be applied to a claim, reconstruction, configuration, trajectory, relation, option field, or bounded praxis scene, provided the type of the referent is declared. The list is illustrative rather than ontologically exhaustive. VECTOR does not require every relevant phenomenon to be redescribed as the same kind of object. It requires only that the current comparison remain clear about what it is an orientation *of*.

This requirement is more important than it first appears. Directional claims are easily strengthened by unnoticed referent drift. An analysis may begin by comparing two claims, then shift to comparing the actors who hold them, then shift again to comparing downstream consequences, while continuing to speak as if one stable object had been under examination throughout. Such movement can be analytically legitimate, but each material change creates a new bounded claim unless continuity is separately established.

Suppose a VECTOR analysis begins with

\[
O_0=\text{a reconstruction of a coordination pattern}.
\]

After a rotation, the analyst begins speaking about

\[
O_1=\text{the institutional role occupied by one participant}.
\]

The second object may be relevant to understanding the first, but it is not automatically the same reference. A directional result about the role cannot simply be imported as evidence that the original reconstruction was superior. The relation between the objects must itself be declared.

This requirement later becomes central to ROT. A rotation that materially changes the reference may still generate orientation, but it cannot silently count as same-claim robustness. The principle is simple:

\[
\boxed{
O_0\rightarrow O_1
\text{ requires an explicit continuity claim if the original claim is to survive.}
}
\]

Reference continuity does not demand rigid identity. Praxis is temporal, relational, and often changing by definition. A trajectory at \(t_1\) is not numerically identical to the same trajectory at \(t_0\) in every respect. A role can change function while remaining historically traceable. An institution can transform while remaining the relevant institutional referent. The requirement is therefore not metaphysical sameness. It is sufficient continuity for the current question.

This makes reference continuity itself question-bound. A change may be irrelevant for one claim and decisive for another. If the question concerns whether a policy retained the same stated objective, substantial changes in implementation may leave the referent sufficiently continuous. If the question concerns the downstream burden created by implementation, the same changes may be constitutive. The relevant question is not *is this exactly the same thing?* but *is this still the same reference for the claim now being tested?*

The human boundary follows immediately. Persons may be participants in a reference object, and patterns of enactment, role relations, capacity, or consequence involving persons may be compared. But the reference object of VECTOR is not intrinsic human worth.

\[
\boxed{
O\neq\text{person worth}.
}
\]

This is not an incidental ethical disclaimer. It is a type boundary. VECTOR's directional relations concern structured praxis under declared questions. A claim about more adequate coordination, more realistic capacity, or more robust correction cannot be converted into a rank of persons without changing the object and claim type. Later discussion of corrigible consequence-carrying praxis will preserve the same distinction. The paper retains “Adult Orientation” only as a supporting shorthand for that stance; neither the stance nor the label becomes a person category or dignity measure.

The first coordinate therefore establishes a basic discipline:

> Before asking what direction a comparison carries, declare what remains the object of that comparison.

## 3.2 Declared Frame

The second coordinate is the declared frame:

\[
F=\text{declared frame}.
\]

The notation must not be confused with the PMS Base operator \(\Box\):

\[
\boxed{F\neq\Box.}
\]

\(\Box\) belongs to the generative grammar. \(F\) is an explicit VECTOR coordinate that states the boundary within which the present orientation claim is made.

A frame can specify which part of a scene is being considered, which roles count as relevant, what temporal interval is included, which institutional or material conditions are held in view, what level of granularity is used, or which functional relation the analysis is attempting to discriminate. The frame need not enumerate every fact about the world. Its purpose is to make the claim's relevant boundary visible enough that later changes can be tracked rather than smuggled in.

The central rule is:

> **Frame change is analytically productive only when it remains visible as change.**

If

\[
F_0\rightarrow F_1,
\]

the resulting analysis may become better, more discriminating, or newly informative. But the gain does not retroactively establish that the original claim under \(F_0\) had already contained the same relation.

This point becomes essential in counterfactual analysis. An analyst may construct a modified frame in which a blocked option becomes available. The modified case can reveal which condition created the blockage. It cannot be used to claim that the option was already available in the original frame. Likewise, a granularity change may expose a relation hidden at coarser resolution. The newly visible relation can motivate revision of the original claim, but the original claim did not survive unchanged merely because a better frame was later found.

VECTOR therefore treats explicit reframing as legitimate and silent reframing as a source of claim inflation.

A frame is not required to be final. Indeed, the possibility that \(F\) is inadequate is one reason ROT (Rotation) exists. But a revisable frame still has to be declared. Otherwise every criticism can be absorbed by changing the boundary after the fact while pretending that the same proposition has been defended throughout.

This is closely related to the later rule that revision is not robustness. If a claim under \(F_0\) fails and a narrower or better-specified claim under \(F_1\) survives, VECTOR should record improvement through revision rather than rewrite the lineage so that the first claim appears to have been robust all along.

The frame coordinate therefore creates a traceable distinction among three different events:

1. the same claim is examined from another perspective while the relevant frame remains sufficiently stable;
2. the frame is deliberately changed as an exploratory probe;
3. the frame is materially revised and a new bounded claim is formed.

Part II will formalize these distinctions through ROT. Chapter 3 needs only the prior condition: frame change must remain visible.

VECTOR makes no general ontology of frames. It does not claim that every scene has one objectively correct boundary independent of question. Frames are functional claim conditions. Their adequacy is tested by what they include, exclude, distort, or make invisible relative to the question at hand.

## 3.3 Question and Claim Scope

The third coordinate is the question:

\[
Q=\text{question / claim scope}.
\]

Without a declared question, direction becomes semantically unstable. A statement such as

\[
X\prec_VY
\]

immediately invites the response: *better in what respect?*

VECTOR treats that response not as a request for optional clarification but as a requirement of the claim itself.

\[
\boxed{
\text{Direction requires a declared question.}
}
\]

The question determines which structural relation the comparison is supposed to discriminate. A reconstruction may preserve asymmetry more truthfully than another while being more complex. A policy may retain more reversibility while creating higher short-term cost. A claim may discriminate a role relation more precisely while carrying less general scope. If the question is left unspecified, these differences can be collapsed into an ungrounded overall judgment.

For this reason, \(Q\) functions as a claim ceiling as well as a topical focus.

Suppose the question is:

> Which reconstruction better preserves the relevant asymmetry for this bounded scene?

A positive result may support a DIR claim regarding preservation of that asymmetry. It does not support the statement:

> This is the better theory overall.

The stronger sentence has changed the question while pretending only to restate the answer.

VECTOR therefore rejects floating superiority claims:

\[
\boxed{
\text{No floating superiority claim.}
}
\]

Every legitimate directional statement is anchored to a comparison burden. This does not make direction arbitrary or merely subjective. The question itself can be badly chosen, too broad, irrelevant, morally loaded, or insufficiently supported. But once declared, it allows the claim to be tested at the level it actually asserts.

The relation between question and scope also helps distinguish local from universal claims. A local question may support a precise directional result under a narrow frame. Expanding the result to another domain, role set, or temporal range is not free generalization. It is a new claim with a larger burden. Later, Chapter 13 will state this as a convergence rule:

\[
Scope\uparrow\Rightarrow Burden\uparrow.
\]

The point already begins here. Scope is not whatever remains after the result has been written. It is part of the conditions under which the result is meaningful.

A question can also legitimately produce no directional relation. Some questions expose trade-offs. Some produce incomparability. Some reveal that the proposed comparison is itself malformed. Declaring \(Q\) therefore does not force a ranking. It makes explicit what would have to be true for ranking to become meaningful.

Chapter 4 will later develop the semantics of local DIR claims. Here the rule is prior and simpler:

> A VECTOR comparison is not well typed until the question that licenses the comparison is visible.

## 3.4 Perspectival Position

The fourth coordinate is the perspectival position:

\[
\boxed{
\pi=\text{perspectival position}.
}
\]

The symbol \(\pi\) is used deliberately rather than \(P\), because MIP already uses \(P\) for power or practical room for action. The distinction avoids a collision between perspective and capacity that would become particularly damaging in Parts III and V.

A perspectival position is a declared relational standpoint with respect to the reference object. It may be the position of an acting role, an affected role, an internal participant, an external observer, an institutional role, or a retrospective standpoint. It may also encode which side of a relation is foregrounded, such as intention or effect. The precise families are developed later through ROT (Rotation).

Perspective in VECTOR is not a theory of personality. It is not identity, worldview, temperament, ideology, moral disposition, or psychological type. Nor does \(\pi\) imply that every claim is reducible to the subjective experience of a perspective-holder. The relevant structures may be real and asymmetrical even where their visibility differs by position.

Two errors are therefore blocked at once.

The first is privilege by default. The initial perspective

\[
\pi_0
\]

has no special epistemic authority merely because it is familiar, institutionally dominant, culturally normal, or analytically convenient. An unrotated view is not a neutral view simply because the rotation has not yet been performed.

The second is relativist flattening. If

\[
\pi_A\neq\pi_B,
\]

it does not follow that the resulting claims are equally warranted, equally informative, or equally adequate.

\[
\boxed{
\pi_A\neq\pi_B
\not\Rightarrow
Claim_A=Claim_B.
}
\]

Perspective variation therefore creates a testing opportunity rather than a presumption of equivalence.

The later ROT (Rotation) theory will make this active. A role rotation may ask whether a DIR (Direction) claim survives movement from actor to affected role. An inside/outside rotation may reveal that internal coherence depends on externalized cost. An intention/effect rotation may show that the declared aim and the realized structural consequence diverge. An Ω-position rotation may expose asymmetrical capacity or exposure that the starting perspective suppressed.

But no rotation creates a view from nowhere. The analyst also occupies a perspectival position. Making \(\pi\) explicit therefore does not transcend perspectival situatedness. It makes situatedness available for inspection.

This produces the central boundary that ROT will later operationalize:

\[
\boxed{
\text{Perspectival freedom does not imply relativism.}
}
\]

A perspective can reveal a real relation without being the only possible access to it. Another perspective can reveal a different real relation without making the first one false. A third can expose that the original directional claim was overgeneralized. VECTOR's task is not to choose a privileged viewpoint in advance but to make such dependencies testable.

## 3.5 Temporal Position

The fifth coordinate is temporal position:

\[
t=\text{temporal position / evaluation index}.
\]

Again, a type boundary is necessary:

\[
\boxed{t\neq\Theta.}
\]

\(\Theta\) is the PMS operator of temporality. The index \(t\) identifies when the VECTOR state or claim is being evaluated.

The distinction is not cosmetic. Orientation is path-sensitive. Between \(t_0\) and \(t_1\), evidence may change, options may appear or disappear, effective capacity may increase or collapse, commitments may harden, publicness may alter reversal cost, institutions may respond, other actors may act, and consequences previously uncertain may become visible. A VECTOR claim that changes across time is therefore not automatically inconsistent.

\[
V_{t_0}\neq V_{t_1}
\]

may simply indicate that the relevant praxis conditions changed.

Temporal indexing is especially important because retrospective knowledge can otherwise be imported silently into earlier evaluations. A later analyst may know that an option existed, that a warning was correct, that a policy failed, or that a downstream effect became severe.
